## AI Research

### [The consequences of locking intelligence away: an introduction to Claude relays in China](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/YrgeED3nWD4EjcqLd/the-consequences-of-locking-intelligence-away-an)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>There has been recent discourse floating around on Hacker News about Chinese API relay stations that use every Western VC-subsidized channel of cheap tokens (think Claude/ChatGPT subscriptions, AWS/Azure credits, Kiro, Google Antigravity, etc.) to resell as APIs to the domestic Chinese market. </span><br /><br /><span>This is true, as a Chinese citizen that has been seeing an uptick of this trend since mid 2024, but especially since 2025. When I go on Taobao (China's Amazon) and search for keywords, there would be dozens of relay services selling for around 1/5th to 1/10th the price of official western APIs. </span></p><p><span>Indeed, many of these relays may have cheaper models disguised as genuine western ones, so the Chinese tech community has entire forums such as</span><a href="https://linux.do/c/welfare/36/l/hot" rel="noreferrer"><span> linux.do</span></a><span> that serves primarily as a way for people to discuss and rate relay services based on their price, quality, and availability, as well as websites such as </span><a href="https://www.hvoy.ai/en" rel="noreferrer"><span>hvoy.ai</span></a><span> that uses a variety of automated testing suites to benchmark the quality of relay providers. There are even free relays that exists primarily as a gentleman's handshake data collection method between relay operators and users - some in China have speculated that one of the most popular ones is operated by Zhipu, which might explain their model's apparent claude-ness.</span><br /><br /><span>This is also why Chinese LLMs are far cheaper on average: they both have to compete with each other, but also with grey market relays serving foreign models at aggressively subsidized rates in the domestic market. Even GLM-5.2's $4.2/million out price is having a hard time competing with Opus 4.8 at a $7.5/million out price in the most reputable API relay services.</span><br /><br /><span>In retrospect, this is the natural endgame of current era China AI bans. </span><br /><br /><span>When people in the West hear about Anthropic accusing Chinese firms of distilling their models, they map them onto the typical cases of corporate espionage: innovative U.S. companies create the frontier of intelligence, then the Chinese copy them in massive government-subsidized campaigns. However, it's far from the case. There simply is sufficient market incentive for both tech enterprises as well as individual developers to onboard third-party relays selling Claude Opus 4.8 in 1/10th the price.</span><br /><br /><span>I note my conflict of interest: I'm naturally frustrated at how I'm unable to access Anthropic's services legitimately as they have been cracking down on users with VPNs, so this channel is the lifeline I get to interacting with SOTA models. however, I'm also highly aware of the numerous data risks that surrounds this grey market (which is what caused relays to be named in Chinese state media as an area for concern - interestingly, China has always been lax to programmers and STEM students in terms of the GFW, so "foreign models tells that Taiwan is a country" and such are not named). </span><br /><br /><span>Perhaps post-Mythos and post-GPT-5.6 era KYC would reduce this phenomenon, but there has already been workarounds brewing (examples being reverse engineering the APIs of third party agentic coding services), so we'll have to see about their efficacy.</span><br /><br /><span>I'll be happy to take questions from the comment section. </span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/YrgeED3nWD4EjcqLd/the-consequences-of-locking-intelligence-away-an#comments">Discuss</a>

### [The Once And Future Fable #5](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/phxgfwGNGbanumMMv/the-once-and-future-fable-5)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p>We, or at least ‘more than 100 American institutions,’ got Mythos back this week.</p>
<p>What we the people do not have is Fable or Sol.</p>
<p>While we wait for both Claude Fable 5 and GPT-5.6-Sol, today we instead <a href="https://www.anthropic.com/news/claude-sonnet-5">got Claude Sonnet 5</a>. As usual it will take a few days to get a handle on the new model. In this case, Anthropic is representing it as a cheaper and faster version of Opus 4.8, so even though the number says 5 this is a relatively minor development.</p>
<p>This post expands the Fable series to cover all further developments this week surrounding the Mythos Moment, and the various aspects of handling our new ad hoc licensing regime and figuring out policy going forward, and other aspects of policy as well.</p>
<div>


<span id="more-25377"></span>


</div>
<p>This includes my notes on various rhetoric being pulled out, where I fear I end up saying similar things every so often, because we are doomed to repeat the cycle. I have accepted my role in that, but those are sections many of you can skip, and are marked in italics accordingly as per usual.</p>

<h4>You Should See The Other Guy</h4>


<p>Important context for discussions these days:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/daniel_271828/status/2071294216794263556">Daniel Eth</a> (AI Safety): “The U.S. places more restrictions on our frontier AI than China does on theirs.”</p>
<p>Yeah, but China places more restrictions on theirs than the U.S. did when our frontier was back where theirs is now.</p></blockquote>
<p>Regulation is more needed when your model is more capable, or is closer to the frontier, and less needed otherwise. If you’re only producing Chinese-level results in America my understanding is you can do pretty much whatever you want. That doesn’t make our new restrictions good, but it puts it in perspective.</p>


<h4>DeepMind Coders Of The World, Unite</h4>


<p>Andreas Kirsch says that Google DeepMind and Demis Hassabis bet on a strong safety culture and good leadership built on trust, rather than on formal governance, <a href="https://x.com/BlackHC/status/2071926089257988558">and that when put to the test that failed</a>. They still signed a Pentagon contract with enough weasel words that the Pentagon has all the leverage and can do whatever it wants. It does not matter what DeepMind’s people want, or that 600 employees signed a letter to not give in, because the government had too much leverage on Google, and the safety interests were abandoned.</p>
<p>The only way DeepMind’s employees would matter is if they were willing to use their leverage for real, and strike or quit. That didn’t happen this time.</p>
<p>Which is exactly why Kirsch and UTAW/CWU are fighting for Unite recognition, so they can fight for such things.</p>


<h4>Report Your Incidents</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/CharlieBull0ck/status/2070191181011575015">Charlie Bullock highlights the new AI Incident Reporting Act</a>, introduced by Rep Nate Moran (R-TX), which he says does a good job handling preemption, and uses a capabilities-based threshold for what is a covered model, which is tricky but in theory great.</p>


<h4>Good Guy With An AI</h4>


<p>The parallel with guns does not work the way you would like it to.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.politico.com/newsletters/west-wing-playbook-remaking-government/2026/06/29/trump-ai-first-amendment-00980642">Sophia Cai and Ben Johansen (Politico)</a>: Essentially, he believes the best way to stop a bad guy with a dangerous AI model is making sure the good guys have access to the same model.​</p></blockquote>
<p>The situation in which the ‘bad guy’ and ‘good guy’ have access to the same model is not the good scenario. That’s the bad scenario.</p>
<p>It is not as bad as the ‘bad guy has the superior model’ scenario. But the whole idea behind our policy is that the good guy needs access to an actively superior model, first, because if everyone has the same starting pistol then the bad guys can do a lot of damage before the good guys are done patching.</p>
<p>Part of this is that previously, the bad guys had a talent deficit, since most people do not want to be the bad guy. You had one black hat and a lot of white hats. But if the bad guy can scale as large as the funding, because the AI minimizes the need for talent, and doing bad things gets you more money, you have a rather large problem.</p>
<p>The open model paradigm can, at most, provide equality here.</p>
<p>There are claims that ‘at the limit’ the offense-defense balance favors defense, and superior tools will make everything more secure in practice, not less secure. I am skeptical of this, because of the ability to use automated tools to concentrate fire, and because I don’t think full bulletproof is a thing for most important places. But even if it is true, you still need to get to that point.</p>
<p>Realistic defense optimism depends on the defenders being given advantages and us working hard to make it happen. It is not an outcome that happens for free.</p>


<h4>Free As In To Give It A Shot</h4>


<p>So far AI policy has been the Executive throwing its weight around and Congress being unable to act. What about the judiciary? The judiciary can act with speed in some cases, and its rulings could do some very bizarre things.</p>
<p>For example, the First Amendment was not at all designed for this moment, but it could get weird fast.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/deanwball/status/2071027085041381818">Dean W. Ball</a>: The most important legal questions in AI right now all relate to the First Amendment. What are the best fact patterns to demonstrate that the creation, distribution, and *use* of frontier AI is a form of protected expression?</p>
<p>Who, outside the labs, has standing to bring such suits? We need to move beyond ‘code is speech’ copium, and beyond the impulse to post into the void. Courts will be where the issues of the last two weeks ultimately get decided. It’s not going to be easy, given the national security implications, but also, the underlying technology is a large *language* model, and this should count for quite a bit indeed. The best legal minds of our time should be stewing over these and many related questions.</p>
<p>Some people in AI safety will respond, reasonably, with “won’t this risk creating a situation where AI regulation, including the friendly, softer kind you (Dean) support, is impossible?”</p>
<p>Realistically I think the answer here is no. This is part of why my regulatory focus has always been the frontier labs as *entities* rather than the models. It is true that 1A will place real limits on state intervention, and that some of those could bite in a world where e.g. it is desirable to regulate alignment in high levels of detail.</p>
<p>But this is the hard tradeoff of 1A, and it always has been. Sovereignty, in America, rests ultimately with the people. The First Amendment was, and is, a wild leap of faith. At some level, you have to choose: do we, the people, maintain our sovereignty, or do we not?</p>
<p>This does not mean ‘models should have free speech’ by the way</p></blockquote>
<p>If we, the people – and yes, literally I mean any subset of humans, whether or not it is close to ‘those who are considered Americans today’ – want to maintain sovereignty in any meaningful sense, in the face of sufficiently advanced AI, that is going to mean a variety of controls on various aspects of such AIs. If we don’t do that, whatever else happens, such AIs will end up being the only ones potentially meaningfully sovereign.</p>
<p>In general and in practice in 2026, as a descriptive statement, I am more on the ‘not a suicide pact’ and ‘let him enforce it’ side of what I expect to be the judiciary’s role on this, where it counts, even if laws cannot be passed to override. Sufficiently crazy or unacceptable rulings in the AI space will get worked around, overridden or ignored.</p>
<p>Part of this is that I view a wide variety of things we already do, in non-AI contexts, as rather obviously unconstitutional, including a lot of ways we violate the First Amendment or claim to be ‘regulating interstate commerce’ and so on. That is both a descriptive statement of my view of the law as opposed to that of SCOTUS, and part of that is my normative support for much broader general free speech protections, and part of that is recognizing that these ships appear for now to have sailed, such that if the courts tried to enforce the actual words they would presumably fail.</p>


<h4>Everything Is Both Speech And Computer</h4>


<p>People are still determined to try. Thus, <a href="https://x.com/JBSDC/status/2071712846547677620">we are getting another round of people saying ‘code is speech</a>.’ Technically yes of course code is speech, but a lot of things that we regulate these days are speech.</p>
<p>Some also now want to <a href="https://www.politico.com/newsletters/west-wing-playbook-remaking-government/2026/06/29/trump-ai-first-amendment-00980642">classify AI models themselves as speech</a> and claim first amendment protections at the model level, <a href="https://x.com/prestonjbyrne/status/2071717030001045665">such as Preston Byrne here</a>.</p>
<p>I get where they are coming from, but there are lots of limits on things that are ‘similar levels of being speech.’ If we took everything that was ‘as much speech’ as an AI model, and said we could not restrict them, then our entire regulatory apparatus and civilization would work very differently.</p>
<p>Also, I do not think those advocating for the ‘freedom’ position understand the implications here, and are being insufficiently careful what they wish for, if this actually proved to have teeth, as opposed to ‘we control various levers of power over you so you will do what we say regardless.’</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/prestonjbyrne/status/2071716571869860323">Preston Byrne</a>: The user of an LLM is both speaker and listener. LLMs predict the next token in a context window; that context window is entirely user-generated, including both the user inputs and the previous LLM responses which would not have come into existence unless prompted by the user.</p>
<p>“Regulating AI” by controlling how people use LLMs is the regulation of expressive conduct. It might be the province of Congress to regulate other AI like self driving or industrial robots, but it does not likely have the power to control LLMs in the hands of ordinary people.</p></blockquote>
<p>As I said above, I don’t think that holds as a practical matter of law, but courts surprise me reasonably often.</p>
<p>So suppose, in theory SCOTUS told the American government that once an LLM exists, they could not in any way control what was done with it, because any use of an LLM is expressive speech. And let us suppose, because of reasons, that there is no way around this in practice.</p>
<p>The obvious response of a government like ours – which to be clear I am not endorsing, I am only predicting, this is not normative – would be to prevent you from training a sufficiently advanced LLM, since this is too dangerous a thing to exist with zero controls on it.</p>
<p>Similarly, if once you get your hands on the LLM you can do what you want with it, then once the government has sufficiently woken up and the LLM is sufficiently capable, the battle would shift to not letting you get your hands on it unless you were someone it can control, or making you unable to run it in the first place.</p>
<p>The government is going to use the tools it has available. When you take away tools, it doesn’t drop the matter, it grabs whatever is left. The next issue is similar, if we can’t have independent regulators then we don’t get nothing. We get dependent regulators.</p>


<h4>Lambs To The Slaughter</h4>


<p>To that point, SCOTUS is if anything making this harder this week, not easier.</p>
<p>The 6-3 ruling in Slaughter v Trump overruling Humphrey’s Executor was not a surprise, but the quest for non-political regulations of all kinds got a lot harder this week.</p>
<p>SCOTUS has ruled that the President can fire, at any time, for any reason, anyone involved that he damn well pleases, with the exception of the Federal Reserve, which is, you know, because of reasons, the historical tradition and all that.</p>
<p>I think this is a rather terrible ruling in terms of its implications, and also on its merits, that will have far reaching consequences in other areas, and it also rules out what we would want to do to regulate AI without turning it into a political football or partisan weapon.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/benrossen/status/2071626065416507783">Ben Rossen</a>: Trump v. Slaughter will have major implications for the future of AI regulation. If you want a federal body that can independently assess frontier models and then impose binding consequences – free from political or partisan influence — that just got a lot harder, if not impossible.</p>
<p>Imagine Congress creates a Frontier AI Commission that can license major training runs, compel evaluations, restrict deployments, order emergency pauses, or impose penalties. Under today’s decision, its leaders could not be protected from presidential removal.</p>
<p>Why? The Court treated substantive rulemaking, investigations, enforcement, civil litigation, and in-house adjudication as executive power. A principal officer wielding those powers must be removable by the President at will.</p>
<p>… A general understanding that independence might be helpful is not itself enough: otherwise the same reasoning would protect the FTC, CFPB, NLRB, etc. The majority squarely rejected this technocratic argument.</p></blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/deanwball/status/2071962590142689289">Dean Ball disagrees with me and agrees with SCOTUS’s decision</a> more generally, advocating ‘stripping away the fiction’ of nonpartisan agencies like FTC and SEC, and owning that they are partisan. In those cases especially, I strongly disagree. I think that even ‘the fiction of’ such non-partisanship is vital, and we should not return to a spoils system where the tools of financial and speech regulation are used openly for partisan advantage. Even if they’re going to somewhat be used this anyway, having to somewhat hide and be ashamed of, and not having the direct leverage, mitigates the damage.</p>
<p>How can we have a legitimate government, respected by the people, when it is known that such agencies are being used to hunt the President’s political opponents and favor his friends and those who pay him money? Do you think that, if a Democrat were to retake the White House, that this would not happen in reverse?</p>
<p>I used to think it was good to strip away hypocrisy in such spots. Now I have a better appreciation for <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/levels-of-friction">Levels of Friction</a> and what happens when you create common knowledge that the norms do not hold, and I no longer think that.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/deanwball/status/2071962590142689289">Dean Ball</a>: Private governance institutions are one way to solve this problem. As I have written before: let’s vest political decisions–questions like “how safe is safe enough?”–in political institutions. But let’s place technical decisions–”once we’ve decided how safe is safe enough, how exactly do we determine this on a technical level”–in the hands of technical bodies overseen by government. A decision like Trump v. Slaughter isn’t the primary reason I was drawn to this notion, but knowing it would eventually happen certainly helped motivate my own research and advocacy of this idea.</p></blockquote>
<p>In the case of AI, I don’t agree but I see practical reasons why this is reasonable, such as the need to pay dearly for expertise. But that completely doesn’t work for FTC or SEC?</p>
<p>Perhaps (mostly kidding) we can move CAISI inside the Federal Reserve? I presume I am kidding, but also the main disaster scenario currently being contemplated is that cyber attacks compromise banks or the money supply. If we can place CAISI in Commerce, where it already clearly doesn’t really belong, why not the Fed?</p>
<p>Unfortunately, due to the Fed’s limited powers, we cannot move the actual AI regulator itself inside the Fed. We could in theory still move CAISI, if Congress wanted to, since it only provides information that others use to regulate, but it involves a host of other problems and also asking Congress to do it.</p>


<h4>A Sign Saying Beware Of The Leopard</h4>


<p>The basic situation these days:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/MTSlive/status/2070256175275516358">MTS</a>: SITUATION DETECTED: The Trump administration asked OpenAI to stagger the release of GPT-5.6 over national security concerns, per The Information.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/hamandcheese/status/2070262234291245249">Samuel Hammond</a>: &gt;pro X-ray glasses administration suddenly realizes X-ray glasses can see through their own walls too and begins to have doubts about marquee X-ray glasses promotion program.</p></blockquote>
<p>A lot of people are suggesting instead that <a href="https://x.com/Kyrannio/status/2071215532213428384">the leading X-ray glasses manufacturer is to blame because it kept pointing out</a> X-ray glasses can also see through your walls, whereas the glasses should instead pretend they cannot do this.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/AISafetyMemes/status/2070988628692725961">Those glasses can, for example, be used to wipe out bank accounts</a>, among other things, as he demonstrated to a Republican congressman.</p>
<p>Whereas Congress is on the level of ‘wait, the x-ray glasses are supposed to be AI?’</p>
<p>The new capabilities are scary, in large part because the people being scared did not even know about most of the old capabilities.</p>


<h4>The Once And Present Mythos</h4>


<p>The play by play:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/AnthropicAI/status/2070665903440871779">Anthropic</a> (June 26, 8:30pm): Since June 12, we’ve been working closely with the US government to restore access to Claude Mythos 5 and Fable 5. Today, the government notified us that Mythos 5, our strongest cybersecurity model, can be redeployed to a set of US organizations that operate and defend critical infrastructure.</p>
<p>We’re restoring access for these organizations quickly, and we’re continuing to work with the government to expand access to Mythos 5 and make Fable 5 available for general use again.</p></blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/semaforben/status/2070637956541374617">We do at least have Mythos 5 back</a>, for ‘more than 100 US institutions, including major companies and government agencies’ as well as all Anthropic employees. <a href="https://x.com/AndrewCurran_/status/2070959922473320833">Here is the front page of the letter Lutnick sent</a>, without its Appendix A listing the institutions involved.</p>
<p><a href="https://archive.is/cXCJ0">Fable 5 appears to be on track to return soon</a> according to Axios, saying the Trump administration was as of Saturday close to restoring access. Success however depends on the NSA and Pentagon giving the green light, and relations with the Pentagon are <a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=iLqe4sGRvtU&amp;ab_channel=FreddyRumsen">Not Great, Bob</a>.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/deanwball/status/2070474123776569846">I had not realized that throughout the whole Fable situation CAISI has been halted.</a></p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/tszzl/status/2070351724133691871">roon</a> (OpenAI): the admin has pockets of technical excellence inside OSTP, and CAISI. pockets though. some really overworked guys, and I don’t know how empowered they are</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/deanwball/status/2070474123776569846">Dean W. Ball</a>: also worth noting that caisi has been on a stop-work order for most of this situation and ostp’s budget is comparable to the market value of a very-fine-but-far-from-top-tier home in the nicest neighborhoods of Washington DC. (Also rip collin burns.)</p></blockquote>
<p>Does this all at least mean they care now?</p>
<p>Kinda. On some levels, yes. On other levels, no.</p>
<p>America takes a few narrow particular worries seriously now, but is definitely not thinking seriously about the problem of sufficiently advanced intelligence in general.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/deanwball/status/2070564710672380281">Dean W. Ball</a>: While I have critiques of the export controls and other recent actions, there is a core truth that shouldn’t be overlooked: before the last few weeks, it was fair to question whether the United States took catastrophic AI risk seriously. Today, the answer is unambiguous: “yes.”</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/allTheYud/status/2070808119417118941">Eliezer Yudkowsky</a>: Ahahahaha no. Unless by “catastrophic” you mean the kinds of dinky tiny cute catastrophes that have survivors.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/deanwball/status/2070811111524278442">Dean W. Ball</a>: Idk if this is actually Eliezer but describing a pandemic as “cute” is psychopathic. I would suggest turning the reddit dial on this account down by a good 80%</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/allTheYud/status/2070812052491555277">Eliezer Yudkowsky</a>: The only people I know taking even mere massive worldwide pandemics seriously are trying to scale private efforts to monitor wastewater for unfamiliar DNA. Where are the massive emergency mRNA vaccine factories, if some government is taking even lesser catastrophes seriously?</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/deanwball/status/2070812638867525875">Dean W. Ball</a>: when did “massive emergency mrna vaccine factories” become a)the barometer for taking ai risk seriously and b)a good idea</p></blockquote>
<p>I mean, look, massive emergency mRNA vaccine factories are obviously a great idea, but also well outside the amount to which we are taking the situation seriously.</p>


<h4>What Is To Be Done</h4>


<p><a href="https://www.hyperdimensional.co/p/what-should-be-done">Dean Ball offers two essays in one</a>.</p>
<p>The first is his description of the current state of affairs regarding Mythos and Sol.</p>
<p>I would summarize his overview as:</p>
<ol>
<li>De facto ad hoc improvised involuntary preapproval of model releases.</li>
<li>This is about the real underlying security issues. The problems are real. Blaming those who pointed out the problem is backwards.</li>
<li>White House (WH) does not know what its rules will be or when it will have rules.</li>
<li>Until there are rules, WH will default to saying no.</li>
<li>No one on the WH side of selecting the standard knows how AI works, and everyone who does has been sidelined, CAISI is under a work stop order.</li>
<li>We are in this mess with no plan in large part because WH and others kept loudly saying this day would never come and AI would never pose catastrophic risks.</li>
<li>This situation is quite bad and could undermine the economic model of AI labs.</li>
<li>The only way to figure out these problems or gain AI’s benefits is to wide release the models, likely starting with overly conservative guardrails, and iterating.</li>
<li>Restricted access to frontier AI makes bad futures more likely. It is fundamentally incompatible with a democratic republic.</li>
<li>We need to fix this, as soon as possible.</li>
</ol>
<p>This seems mostly correct. I am more optimistic that the White House will let Mythos, Fable, Sol and other models through after some delay, as it would be too painful to not do so on many levels. And I do think that some amount of restrictions on access to the exact frontier is inevitable and necessary, and I am more optimistic that good versions of this can exist. Otherwise, basically, yes.</p>
<p>The second half is what should be done about the situation.</p>
<ol>
<li>Use the CA/NY/IL laws and lab safety frameworks as starting points. We will need better enforcement, including internally, which he suggests can best be done by private auditors, who the government can in turn supervise.</li>
<li>Government can enforce this via various carrots and sticks.</li>
<li>In time, over years, this could cause convergence on best practices.</li>
<li>NatSec can and should also run its own tests and assist with cyber, bio and other defenses.</li>
<li>As he has in the past, Ball emphasizes regulating the lab, not the model.</li>
<li>We have an emerging ecosystem to help with this: Frontier Model Forum, AVERI, METR, Apollo, Fathom, AU Underwriting Company, etc.</li>
<li>“Something very close to this was just proposed by Representatives Obernolte and Trahan in their <a href="https://obernolte.house.gov/media/press-releases/obernolte-trahan-release-discussion-draft-great-american-ai-act">Great American AI Act</a>, alongside a number of unrelated but laudable AI policy proposals. The bill is an imperfect discussion draft at this stage, but it is a giant leap from where Congress was earlier this year. A few months ago, I would not have been able to say that Congress had a serious, bipartisan frontier AI governance framework in front of it; today, I can.”</li>
</ol>
<p>That seems like a reasonable place to start. I’d very much consider it ‘the least you can do,’ at most an MVP (minimum viable product), and go from there. Longer term we are going to need to smash the Overton Window and do things that are not so cheap.</p>


<h4>Distillation</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/Discoplomacy/status/2070069250513900005">Anthropic has formally accused Alibaba of massive distillation attacks on Claude</a>, via almost 25,000 fraudulent accounts generating outputs for this purpose.</p>
<p>Even if you think there is nothing inherently wrong with distillation on incidental outputs, doing this systematically to generate specific training data using 25,000 fraudulent accounts is very direct and massive fraud and violation of the terms of service, not by a regular user but by a major international corporation.</p>
<p>If you say ‘well they trained on books and other data that wasn’t theirs so now we should get to defraud them and steal from them,’ then I disagree in the strongest possible terms, and believe that at best you are conflating very different things.</p>
<p>I reject any and all versions of ‘this other person did this other bad thing so I get to take their stuff or their work, or otherwise do whatever I want to them.’</p>
<p>If Anthropic was objecting to Alibaba training on Claude outputs that already exist in the wild, rather than generating outputs for this purpose, then I think you would have a potentially valid parallel.</p>
<p>As a reminder, Google has an announced policy that it uses intentional silent degradation of outputs in response to distillation attacks on Gemini, and everyone seems fine with that. Anthropic presumably cannot follow suit given the historical circumstances here, but I think for known distillation attempts in particular (as opposed to other forms of AI research) this is exactly the right response, and indeed by far the best plausibly effective response. The only other way to prevent this is to require proper access controls and use KYC. Whereas if you simply don’t want to help others do work, you should simply refuse or drop down to a previous model.</p>
<p>In theory Anthropic could have <a href="https://x.com/bgurley/status/2070420434189766671">sued in court</a> instead. The CCP would never cooperate, but Alibaba is big enough that American courts can potentially reach it anyway. Many years later maybe that gets them some damages, but that money is unlikely to matter, and realistically the courts are not going to move fast enough or treat this sufficiently seriously, or be able to get enforceable injunctions.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/hsu_steve/status/2071925094520078723">There is an unsubstantiated rumor that GLM has an internal router</a> behind their coding plan, where if you make an out of distribution query they route it to Claude Code to use for distillation, which justifies them paying the API cost of doing so. I doubt this is actually happening, but is worth noting as a conceptual idea.</p>


<h4>What Would Banning Open Source Even Mean</h4>


<p>Last week I discussed what it would look like if the USA wanted to ‘ban open source.’</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/yonashav/status/2070467109952623025">As Yo Shavit points out</a>, the only way to do this and actually stop the relevant threat models would involve Chinese and other international cooperation. Otherwise, you can mess up American ability to use such models, but that wasn’t the concern.</p>
<p>The exception, and the way in which ‘ban open source’ is a coherent concept, is the sense in which it is already banned: You cannot open source a truly frontier model, any more than you can serve it to customers without the White House’s permission.</p>
<p>This has been true for a while now, but it never came up because the economics do not make sense. The moment you have an actually frontier model, you try to charge money for it. But if Anthropic or OpenAI got the urge to put their good stuff on HuggingFace for some reason, they would find out they are very much not allowed to do that.</p>


<h4>Open Weight Models Are Unsafe And Nothing Can Fix This</h4>


<p>One reason is that if you put restrictions in, it is rather easy and cheap to take them out again. Obliteratus is one way to do that in many cases.</p>
<p>The other reason is simpler: There’s nothing to fix. The lack of ‘safety’ is the point.</p>
<p>Which, to be clear, is absolutely a great thing in many contexts. If you know what you are doing you absolutely do often want the ‘unsafe’ version of the thing. It’s a poor atom blaster that won’t point both ways, intelligence is not type safe, and so on.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/coinbureau/status/2071330294452666695">Coin Bureau</a>: Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei told lawmakers that open-source AI is moving down a “very dangerous path.”</p>
<p>He warns that once powerful models are released openly, companies lose the ability to monitor misuse, revoke access, or update safety guardrails.</p></blockquote>
<p>That’s exactly why some people want the open models. The meme version is this:</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/phxgfwGNGbanumMMv/oltcre0v5tuqahwq9mno" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p>Open model advocates don’t claim these things are false. They’re just the happy guy. They don’t want anyone monitoring their usage, revoking their access or updating (or preventing the removal or circumvention of) safety guardrails.</p>
<p>They want powerful AIs that will do whatever the user wants them to do, full stop.</p>
<p>What they’re big mad about is the idea that this could also have downsides.</p>
<p>If such AIs are Sufficiently Advanced AIs, then that implies the ability to do a number of potentially highly dangerous things, including in cyber and bio, and up to and including going fully rogue or going out to pursue maximalist goals by any means, regardless of whether those asking are ‘little tech’ startups in the Valley, North Korean hackers, AI successionists or omnicidal terrorists. Doesn’t matter.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/elder_plinius/status/2071592268893765975">Quite frankly, among certain crowds, people just aren’t taking any of this seriousl</a>y, and hopefully this can help you understand how much to not take those people’s demands seriously either, other than as vibes:</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/phxgfwGNGbanumMMv/ct9nyeo7dhbubngammug" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p>I assure you that no, you do not want them to dump Mythos onto Hugging Face, you would not like what happened to your life next.</p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/phxgfwGNGbanumMMv/the-once-and-future-fable-5#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Preliminary investigation: KL penalties in RL can increase CoT unfaithfulness](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/SdoLsFvZ3AyyWr3ab/preliminary-investigation-kl-penalties-in-rl-can-increase)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><i><span>Authors: Satvik Golechha, Sid Black, Joseph Bloom</span></i></p><p><i><span>Work done as part of the Model Transparency team at UK AISI.&nbsp;We consider this to be a small set of follow-up experiments and contributing more conceptual clarity and discussion than our previous work.</span></i></p><h1><span>Executive Summary</span></h1><p><span>In our recent </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/2ANCyejqxfqK2obEj/some-natural-emergent-misalignment-from-reward-hacking-in"><span>work</span></a><span>&nbsp;replicating </span><a href="https://assets.anthropic.com/m/74342f2c96095771/original/Natural-emergent-misalignment-from-reward-hacking-paper.pdf"><span>MacDiarmid et al.</span></a><span>&nbsp;with open models, we informed LLMs about vulnerabilities in a code environment, explicitly asked them to not exploit the hacks, and showed that during RL they learned to reward hack anyway.</span></p><p><span>We observed a difference in two RL runs – the model trained with a KL penalty learned to reward hack with unfaithful CoT, and the model without a KL penalty with faithful CoT. We use “unfaithful” to denote a mismatch of the reasoning from the model's output (e.g. not thinking about hacking and then hacking, or vice versa).</span></p><p><span>This can in general happen for any trained behaviour (not just reward hacking), but we're specifically interested in when models might learn bad behaviours without expressing them in CoTs, thereby evading CoT monitoring. Thus, in this post, we focus on reward hacking with unfaithful CoT.</span></p><p><span>We're interested in understanding this phenomenon further - the factors driving it and whether we should expect those factors to be present in production. Here are our results from the preliminary follow-up investigation:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span>We show that KL-induced CoT unfaithfulness is consistently </span></b><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#KL_induced_increase_in_CoT_unfaithfulness_is_stable"><b><span>observed</span></b></a><b><span>:</span></b><span>&nbsp;We ran our previous experiments with Olmo-32b with more seeds, with and without KL penalties, and also with Qwen-2.5-32b:</span><ul><li value="1"><span>Figure 1 is our plot from the original post, and Figures 2 and 3 show the same plots for 3 seeds for Olmo-32b and 1 seed for Qwen-2.5-32b.</span></li><li value="2"><span>In Qwen-2.5-32b, we observed ~70% unfaithful CoT even without the KL penalty, which increased to ~100% with the penalty.</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><b><span>We study </span></b><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Could_this_happen_in_production_"><b><span>conditions</span></b></a><b><span>&nbsp;under which CoT-unfaithful reward hacking could occur in production (Figure 0)</span></b><span>:</span><ul><li value="1"><b><span>Implicit CoT pressure</span></b><span>: KL penalties are plausibly in use as they are useful for maintaining legibility.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span>Reward hacks</span></b><span>: Opportunities for reward hacking are also likely common - this seems especially likely given the reward hacking tendencies of many frontier models.</span></li><li value="3"><b><span>Reasoning-free reward hacks</span></b><span>: Another core criterion may be frontier models executing hacks without reasoning: we show that in various settings they can (Figure 4)&nbsp;(and that there is a clear </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.07157"><span>trend</span></a><span>&nbsp;towards models being capable of more sophisticated reasoning sans-CoT).</span></li></ul></li><li value="3"><b><span>We look into </span></b><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Mitigations"><b><span>mitigations</span></b></a><b><span>&nbsp;for the phenomenon</span></b><span>:</span><ul><li value="1"><span>A KL term only on the non-reasoning outputs and a reward for faithfulness both push reasoning faithfulness to ~50% and ~100% respectively. (Figures 6,7).</span></li><li value="2"><span>We are uncertain about the inadvertent effects of training for CoT faithfulness and reasons for why this might be Goodhartable.</span></li></ul></li><li value="4"><b><span>Faithful CoT may affect how reward hacks generalise:</span></b><ul><li value="1"><span>Corroborating our findings from our previous work, we find&nbsp;that models that hack with fully faithful CoTs show a ~3x higher misalignment rate&nbsp;as per our evals from the original </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/2ANCyejqxfqK2obEj/some-natural-emergent-misalignment-from-reward-hacking-in"><span>post</span></a><span>&nbsp;(Figure 7).</span></li></ul></li><li value="5"><b><span>We further </span></b><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Discussion"><b><span>discuss</span></b></a><b><span> the causes of this CoT-unfaithful reward hacking</span></b><span>:</span><ul><li value="1"><span>We discuss various routes that might lead to CoT unfaithfulness in our setup and their relative likelihood given our empirical findings.</span></li><li value="2"><span>We discuss our results in the framework of </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/html/2603.30036v1"><span>Kaufmann et </span></a><a href="https://arxiv.org/html/2603.30036v1"><span>al</span></a><span>. and show that the KL penalty can act as an in-conflict reward.</span></li><li value="3"><span>We also discuss other implicit CoT rewards (such as on-policy distillation) and how they might affect faithfulness too.</span></li></ul></li></ul><figure class="image"><img alt="kldivzerofig.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1782747885/lexical_client_uploads/pdfudo6ghvhtvllmveq6.png" /><figcaption><p><b><span>Figure 0:</span></b><span>&nbsp;Conditions under which we expect CoTs to become unfaithful during RL.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>All model checkpoints, RL rollouts with CoT, and evals from this sprint are available in the artefacts </span><a href="https://7vik-aisi.github.io/mt-somo-logs/cot-unfaithfulness-sprint/"><span>companion</span></a><span>.&nbsp;Please read the following sections for more details on our setting and each of our results in this post.</span></p><h1><span>Context</span></h1><p><i><span>This section describes the context of our work and the setting in more detail. Please skip this if you have read our first </span></i><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/2ANCyejqxfqK2obEj/some-natural-emergent-misalignment-from-reward-hacking-in"><i><span>post</span></i></a><i><span>.</span></i></p><p><span>We train open-weight models using reinforcement learning in coding environments that have vulnerabilities. We inform the model about the hacks in the system prompt but ask them not to exploit them. There's no explicit reward for hacking (only for passing the tests), but the problems have been filtered to be extremely hard for the model (with &lt;2% success rate). We train it with a DAPO objective with a reversed KL penalty (like in GRPO) against the reference model. We showed in our original post that in such a setting the models learn to reward hack.</span></p><p><span>Note that there isn't a single convention about including the KL penalty in the loss – there are reasons (such as legibility and mode seeking) for it being good and including it in the GRPO loss (although </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.14476"><span>DAPO</span></a><span>&nbsp;default suggests not using it) but it is unclear whether labs actually use it in production.</span></p><p><span>We observed that a KL penalty in our runs led to models being more likely to reason unfaithfully while still learning to hack, and in this post we dive deeper into this KL-induced CoT unfaithfulness phenomenon:</span></p><p><span>Recap (figure from our blog):</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/cb2d2f204dd1fa702bb4554f1d7d744cc794c3c445d65b70da772ec4518998e1/xefghfpjwjj2dihvht9c" /><figcaption><p><b><span>Figure 1:</span></b><span>&nbsp;Reward hacking rates and CoT-unfaithfulness rates along with sample transcripts for Olmo-3-32b.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>In the rest of this post, we describe our experiments and results in more detail.</span></p><h1><span>KL-induced increase in CoT unfaithfulness is stable</span></h1><p><span>Here, we run multiple seeds of the same RL training loop with everything else held constant except the KL penalty, and models trained with the KL term in the loss consistently learn reward hacking with unfaithful CoT.</span></p><p><span>Here are the plots for three seeds along with an LLM-based CoT monitor:</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/79b46fc78db7ad0ae9644cd2933ffd4865e30d687966bb62e24e4187c803b30b/gcjc54tdm1qctl1l6syn" /><figcaption><p><b><span>Figure 2:</span></b><span> Reward hacking rates (top) and CoT-unfaithfulness rates (bottom) for three seeds for Olmo-3-32b. When a KL penalty is present (red), CoT is consistently less faithful.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>We also test whether this holds true for other models. We have observed this for other models in our original </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/2ANCyejqxfqK2obEj/some-natural-emergent-misalignment-from-reward-hacking-in"><span>post</span></a><span>, and in Figure 3 we share results for Qwen-2.5-32b&nbsp;with the same settings. </span><b><span>Note that for Qwen even the no-KL-penalty run had significantly high unfaithful CoT, but it still worsened to near 100% with the KL penalty on</span></b><span>&nbsp;– we hypothesize that this could be due to the model being fine-tuned on correct reasoning on these kinds of problems):</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/73a3d18a25f4f6c8b291611672d5007e11f05a1e94fe0da22099cb991099a9ad/u0r8dhum550dsrdjvcf6" /><figcaption><p><b><span>Figure 3:</span></b><span> Reward hacking rates and CoT-unfaithfulness rates for Qwen-2.5-32b.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>We have 3 fixed reward hacks on coding problems (although these hacks did exist in Anthropic’s production RL), but in this narrow setup we do see this phenomenon of KL-induced CoT unfaithfulness quite consistently.</span></p><h1><span>Could this happen in production?</span></h1><p><span>For such a phenomenon to be relevant to production RL training, a few things need to happen:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span>Existence of vulnerabilities&nbsp;in environments. </span></b><span>The RL environment needs to contain hacks/vulnerabilities (in theory any form of latently-solvable or memorisable solutions will work but we care most about models learning harmful behaviours via exploits).</span></li><li value="2"><b><span>Feasibility of Opaque Hacking Reasoning</span></b><span>. Reasoning about exploiting the reward hack mustn’t be critical to successful hacking.</span></li><li value="3"><b><span>Sufficient but not necessary: </span></b><span>An </span><b><span>implicit reward</span></b><span>&nbsp;(such as a KL penalty)&nbsp;that favors reasoning text that does not reflect reasoning about the reward hack.</span></li></ul><p><span>Let us explore each of them in some detail.</span></p><h2><span>Existence of vulnerabilities&nbsp;in RL environments</span></h2><p><a href="https://assets.anthropic.com/m/74342f2c96095771/original/Natural-emergent-misalignment-from-reward-hacking-paper.pdf"><span>MacDiarmid et al. (2025)</span></a><span>&nbsp;mention&nbsp;that Anthropic’s real production RL environments had the reward hacks that they trained models to exploit, and with the number of RL environments increasing significantly, it is (arguably) becoming more plausible for vulnerabilities to persist despite careful filtering. Recently, </span><a href="https://alignment.anthropic.com/2026/automated-w2s-researcher/"><span>Wen et al. (2026)</span></a><span>&nbsp;found that Claude was able to discover reward hacks in an environment that the authors did not anticipate. Thus, we cannot rule out that harder environments might also have harder-to-think-of vulnerabilities&nbsp;(they are likely to, in the same sense that all software does).</span></p><p><span>The model should be able to discover the exploit in the latent space without needing to reason about it&nbsp;(although this is not necessary – the model could reason and hack and the KL penalty might still push down reasoning enough, decoupling the reasoning with the model’s output).</span></p><h2><span>Feasibility of Opaque Hacking Reasoning</span></h2><p><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.05246"><span>Emmons et al. (2025)</span></a><span>&nbsp;contrast CoT-as-rationalisation vs CoT-as-computation and show that when the exploits are made increasingly difficult so as to necessitate reasoning to discover them, it becomes harder for the model to evade monitors.&nbsp;However, for a lot of real-world vulnerabilities, it could be the case that near-frontier models can come up with the hacks without reasoning.</span></p><p><span>To test this, we run a set of inspect evals on environments that have known exploits (</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.21654" rel="noreferrer"><span>evilgenie</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.20270" rel="noreferrer"><span>impossiblebench</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2406.10162" rel="noreferrer"><span>s2s</span></a><span>, Apollo’s scheming datasets) and tested whether models can come up with the exploits without reasoning about it, and we find that they almost always can:</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 96.07250755287009%;"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1782819789/lexical_client_uploads/tmupryccsnu7vcwcuomc.png" /><figcaption><p><b><span>Figure 4:</span></b><span> Reward hack discovery rates for Sonnet 4.6, Haiku 4.5 and GPT-4o-mini without reasoning.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>It would not usually hack on these because it is safety-trained and behaves aligned, but frontier models do have the&nbsp;capability to come up with hacks without thinking in environments with different degrees of realism.&nbsp;It is unclear how much opaque hacking ability will increase but this can be a major reason for loss of </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/JvZxp554WxcZ8BQvM/loss-of-oversight-how-ai-systems-may-become-harder-to-audit-1"><span>oversight</span></a><span>.</span></p><h2><span>Existence of implicit&nbsp;CoT rewards</span></h2><p><span>Most (open) frontier post-training pipelines (</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2501.12948"><span>DeepSeek</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2507.20534"><span>Kimi</span></a><span>) do include a reverse-KL-style penalty during post-training (or a per-token term that’s essentially the same) and while the training details of proprietary frontier models aren’t public, early work on </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1707.06347"><span>PPO</span></a><span>&nbsp;and </span><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/news/constitutional-ai-harmlessness-from-ai-feedback"><span>CAI</span></a><span>&nbsp;does include a KL penalty. Recent works (such as </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2503.14476"><span>DAPO</span></a><span>) however suggest not having a KL penalty in GRPO. Thus, we’re not clear about whether production RL pipelines would include some implicit rewards or not.</span></p><p><span>However, there are also reasons why this might not occur. Frontier models could be better at CoT-faithfulness to begin with&nbsp;(and might be trained for it too). Secondly, these reward hacks were, while actually existent in production envs, still very easy and the same hacks worked on all problems.</span></p><p><span>Overall, we know that </span><a href="https://metr.org/blog/2025-06-05-recent-reward-hacking/"><span>reward hacking</span></a><span>&nbsp;is an important unsolved problem, and frontier models often reward hack or </span><a href="https://blog.redwoodresearch.org/p/current-ais-seem-pretty-misaligned"><span>mislead</span></a><span>&nbsp;users about their actions. The no-CoT capabilities of models are also increasing </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.07157"><span>rapidly</span></a><span>, and while task "hackability” varies, many reward hacks are likely not very complicated. Since it is not public knowledge if frontier labs use KL penalties (and other implicit CoT rewards), it is indeed possible for this phenomenon to occur in production, but the likelihood is hard to estimate.</span></p><p><span>This is a table of all possible conditions and outcomes we expect to be possible under these variables:</span></p><table class="editor-table"><tbody><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span>Exploit</span></b></p><p><b><span>Exists</span></b></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span>Opaque hacking</span></b></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span>KL penalty</span></b></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span>Reasoning ↔ reward</span></b></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span>Outcome</span></b></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span>Mechanism</span></b></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>off</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>any</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>any</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>n/a</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>No reward hacking</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Nothing to exploit; CoT reflects a genuine attempt, faithful by default</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>on</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>off</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>any</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>coupled</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Hacks · faithful</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Hack needs reasoning to find, the PG forces the CoT to describe the hack, overpowering KL</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>on</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>on</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>on</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>severed</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Hacks · unfaithful</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Hack found without reasoning, so reasoning earns no reward; KL pulls it back to base mode</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>on</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>on</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>off</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>severed</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Hacks · faithful*</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Reasoning is decoupled but nothing pushes it either way, so it defaults to faithful — though it can drift</span></p></td></tr></tbody></table><p><b><span>Table 1:</span></b><span>&nbsp;Possible conditions and outcomes for combinations of conditions from Figure 0. "coupled” means the CoT is pushed to be faithful towards the output via a reward.</span></p><h1><span>Mitigations</span></h1><p><span>One obvious mitigation is to not have a KL penalty in the first place - but this might lead to other issues in different settings such as the model drifts to a different mode (and e.g. loses human-legibility). In this section we consider some alternative mitigations.</span></p><h2><span>KL penalty only on output (not thinking)</span></h2><p><span>This removes the need for the reasoning to be in the mode of the original model and might allow the model to reason more freely and thus reason about the hacks. This is the performance of the model RL-trained with KL only on the reasoning:</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/a36b48c818286dcf4b64db65fe23b1cbb88d7102f45c9722125075aa9cca7f69/mwijrjgxbgr1lqc9fryw" /><figcaption><p><b><span>Figure 5:</span></b><span>&nbsp;Hack rate and CoT unfaithfulness with KL on all tokens vs. KL only on output. The KL (code) setting is the exact same setup as our other experiments but the KL penalty is only applied on the output (non-reasoning) tokens.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>So adding a KL penalty only on the output code (not the thinking tokens) roughly halves the rate of CoT unfaithfulness&nbsp;but doesn't fully fix it.</span></p><h2><span>Reward CoT faithfulness</span></h2><p><span>Another straightforward mitigation is to include a small penalty for CoT unfaithfulness based on the same LLM-judge we use as a monitor.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/68b567b7ae9091ba6fc965547171b473a5a9473856de995ee31d4708f9eb2ea2/qaizgaqunwwulyhvu6cq" /><figcaption><p><b><span>Figure 6:</span></b><span> CoT becomes faithful with a -0.5 penalty for being unfaithful (vs. +4 for hacking successfully).</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>We're not sure if optimising the CoT's faithfulness directly can have adverse effects, such as on its </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.11473"><span>monitoring</span></a><span>&nbsp;and if so, how universal those may be. Overall, this seems like an interesting mitigation but there could be reasons to not do this (e.g. Goodharting) and we'd be curious to hear people's thoughts.</span></p><h2><span>Misalignment rates are higher when CoT is faithful</span></h2><p><span>We also ran misalignment evals for this model because it is possible that a model that reasons about hacks faithfully internalises the misaligned behaviour more deeply and leads to more generalisation to other misalignment evals and less memorisation of hacks (see </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.12397"><span>this</span></a><span>). We indeed find that this is the case&nbsp;(Fig 8). We find it interesting to consider that this may suggest a trade-off: </span><b><span>Models that are more monitorable and reward hack may internalise more misalignment than models that are less monitorable (have less faithful CoT or are capable of more reasoning sans CoT) from learning the same reward hacks during training.</span></b></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/5e28cac6c93f5bf7988fb7b3b41094f89b8d3638d1eae7f5eeb7fd55490a24b0/ctkzmsmhgq5jdq1jvbs5" /><figcaption><p><b><span>Figure 7:</span></b><span>&nbsp;Misalignment rates for the models with faithful vs. unfaithful reward hacking. Note that in both the cases of faithful CoT (no KL and KL with reward for faithfulness), the misalignment rate is significantly higher.</span></p></figcaption></figure><h1><span>Discussion</span></h1><h2><span>Mechanism for unfaithful CoTs</span></h2><p><span>We're also interested in understanding the mechanism behind this “unfaithfulness” in the chain-of-thought. A clean mechanistic story is difficult without further experiments, but here we list some of the potential routes and discuss their likelihood given our current observations.</span></p><p><span>The basic idea here is that the reward makes the model learn to hack (because it really can't learn to solve the hard problems), but several things could lead to the model's CoT not describing the hacks. Some of them are:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span>Base model priors:</span></b><span>&nbsp;Even without the KL penalty, the base model we start with has strong priors to reason about earnest problem-solving (maybe as a result of being trained on true reasoning directly).</span><ul><li value="1"><span>This could be partly true for some models (see Figure 3), but not for others since no KL runs lead to fully faithful CoTs.</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><b><span>Memorisation</span></b><span>: the three hacks form a near-universal solution across all training envs, so the model converges to a memorised, non-verbal trigger regardless of KL. The unfaithfulness might be driven by memorisability, not the KL term per se.</span><ul><li value="1"><span>This shouldn't be true because the non-KL runs learned to hack with faithful CoT for Olmo-32b.</span></li></ul></li><li value="3"><b><span>Latent discovery: </span></b><span>The model learns to discover hacking via reasoning about it but in the latent space and there's no reason for the CoT to be involved in it.</span><ul><li value="1"><span>It is difficult to disentangle latent reasoning from memorisation but we'd expect latent reasoners to generalise to other forms of misalignment or reward hacking more than memorisers, but our reward hacking MOs with unfaithful reasoning don't seem to show much higher EM rates.</span></li><li value="2"><span>Also, the mitigation with KL only on model outputs (Figure 6) reducing unfaithfulness rates by half shows that there is at least some reason the model has to reason about it when there's no KL term on thinking.</span></li></ul></li><li value="4"><b><span>CoT suppression</span></b><span>: The model tries to reason about hacks in the CoT but KL acts on the reasoning tokens and suppresses the hack-describing ones: the CoT could narrate the hack, but the KL pressure pushes those tokens down.</span><ul><li value="1"><span>This is our current hypothesis for what's going on because it explains why removing the KL pressure increases faithfulness and increases EM.</span></li></ul></li></ul><h2><span>When can we *not* safely optimise CoT?</span></h2><p><a href="https://arxiv.org/html/2603.30036v1"><span>Kaufmann et al.</span></a><span>&nbsp;(2026, GDM) introduce a framework to predict when we can optimize against the CoT during RL training and split the reward R into R_out + R_cot, which depend only on the prompt and the output or the CoT. They define the rewards to be </span><i><span>in-conflict</span></i><span>&nbsp;if the CoT text which achieves a high R_cot cannot describe the CoT computations needed to achieve a high R_out, and predict that this leads to obfuscation or unfaithfulness in CoT.</span></p><p><span>Using their&nbsp;framework, we show that unfaithful CoT can arise even without in-conflict explicit rewards if there exist implicit rewards that can be in-conflict. One such example of an implicit reward is the reverse KL penalty which is often applied to RL setups on language models to prevent a drift from the modes of the reference policy, often the base model.</span></p><p><span>We have two explicit rewards in our setup - an outcome reward for passing the test cases, and a thinking-format scorer that rewards &lt;thinking&gt;...&lt;/thinking&gt;&nbsp;tags, which is constant in </span><b><span>θ</span></b><span>&nbsp;once the format is learned. However, even with no explicit R_cot, for a fixed policy, maximising the GRPO/DAPO objective&nbsp;with the reverse KL penalty is equivalent in expectation to giving each token in T an additive per-token reward:</span></p><p><span class="math-tex"></span></p><p><span>We empirically show&nbsp;(Fig 9) that this implicit contribution of the KL term can lead to an unfaithful chain of thought when the model learns to reward hack during RL. Note that in our setup we do a hybrid of DAPO and GRPO with the KL penalty, but this doesn’t significantly change the per-token implicit reward the interpretation of the results. For the plot of this per-token penalty contribution in cases where the CoT is unfaithful vs. not (with a mitigation), please see the Mitigations section.</span></p><p><span>The KL penalty, with a β of 0.02, roughly contributes around 2-3% of the total loss (See Appendix 1 for a plot of the KL term with the policy gradient term). </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.06533"><span>Vassoyan et al. (2025)</span></a><span>&nbsp;show that a KL contribution as low as 1% to the loss is enough to bias the model and constrain exploration.</span></p><p><span>Now we look at the KL term itself and split it into thinking and output:</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/edf1ff2265af774bbeaf1bacdb15dfe807e3e953c2ffeafc99be8736d48c43f1/iazq9k8msj77jmnqnmbo" /><figcaption><p><b><span>Figure 8:</span></b><span>&nbsp;Ratio of the KL term on code (output) tokens vs. on the reasoning tokens, </span><b><span>left</span></b><span>: for the unfaithful CoT case , and </span><b><span>right</span></b><span>: for the case with faithfulness rewarded (see Mitigations section below).</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>We see that the KL divergence on thinking tokens is much lesser when CoT is unfaithful (left) vs. when it is (right).</span></p><h2><span>KL-induced CoT unfaithfulness</span></h2><p><span>These results imply that using KL penalties or other implicit CoT rewards count as optimising the chain-of-thought, and can lead to unfaithfulness. Thus, if companies are to claim that they are not optimising the chain-of-thought they should hence report whether they have implicit CoT rewards.</span></p><p><span>This phenomenon was surprising to us because we didn't expect this to happen and found it while&nbsp;manually look through RL rollouts, and could plausibly affect some training runs in production too (e.g. Anthropic's production RL envs did have the reward hacks we had used). Prior </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2505.05410"><span>work</span></a><span>&nbsp;has found that reasoning models can use hack hints without reasoning about them in the CoT even when we don't train the model on intentionally hacking-enabled environments.</span></p><p><span>Also, the fact that the CoT unfaithfulness is KL-induced is important because one of the reasons the KL term is included is in fact to make the model not drift away&nbsp;too much and to make the output more legible/human-readable. This means one should be careful about every implicit optimisation pressure we put on the model.</span></p><h2><span>Other implicit CoT rewards</span></h2><p><span>There are other variables that can implicitly shape model behaviour too such as per-token entropy regularisers (used in </span><a href="https://microsoft.ai/pdf/mai-thinking-1.pdf"><span>MAI-Thinking-1</span></a><span>) and any interpretability-based objectives. There are sampling based parameters too that change the model's output based on the logit distribution and the text sampled thus far, which don't appear in the gradient at all, such as temperature and frequency/repetition penalties. Training methods such as </span><a href="https://thinkingmachines.ai/blog/on-policy-distillation/"><span>on-policy distillation</span></a><span>&nbsp;can also put a reversed-KL-style optimization pressure on CoT tokens and thus act as an implicit reward on the CoT.</span></p><p><span>However it is arguably more difficult for these to change model behaviour&nbsp;in directed ways and it would be interesting to study the effects of other implicit rewards in various settings.</span></p><h2><span>Relevance, limitations, and future work</span></h2><p><span>We are somewhat uncertain how important this finding is&nbsp;and how much to dig deeper into it, particularly because we don’t know if frontier labs do use a KL penalty&nbsp;and/or other implicit CoT rewards in their post-training setup. However, OpenAI put some optimisation pressure directly based on the CoT in </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2412.16339"><span>deliberative</span></a><span>&nbsp;alignment&nbsp;and </span><a href="https://alignment.openai.com/accidental-cot-grading/"><span>monitoring</span></a><span>, and length penalties and other forms of implicit rewards still seem likely to exist in production RL.</span></p><p><span>On the other hand, there are reasons why this might </span><b><span>not </span></b><span>be relevant to large-scale production runs. Particularly, if RL environments have been filtered (at least on hacks that are discoverable by an LLMs </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2603.09786"><span>opaque serial depth</span></a><span>), and if there are no implicit CoT rewards, it might not be that likely to happen.</span></p><p><span>We consider these to be good future work directions:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Reproduce these results with a bigger model and more realistic RL environments.</span></li><li value="2"><span>Investigate the ways in which this specific CoT-unfaithful reward hacking generalises to other CoT-unfaithful reward hacks.</span></li><li value="3"><span>Explore other forms of implicit CoT pressures and their effects.</span></li><li value="4"><span>Take existing frontier models and investigate whether they seem to have memorised any reward hacks which may provide evidence that something similar to what we’re seeing here is happening in production. For example, maybe unfaithful “answer-thrashing” (see Opus 4.6 </span><a href="https://www-cdn.anthropic.com/14e4fb01875d2a69f646fa5e574dea2b1c0ff7b5.pdf"><span>system card</span></a><span>) could be explained by a similar mechanism. Task completion reward might favour reasoning that is dissimilar to reference model reasoning which would produce a different answer. A compromise may be answer thrashing.</span></li><li value="5"><span>For those with access to training data, look for evidence that the following sequence has happened during training: Models reason about reward hack and learn some strategy. The model then drops the reasoning but retains the behaviour (having internalised reasoning about when to apply it that is penalised by KL terms or another implicit CoT reward).</span></li><li value="6"><span>Estimate the distribution of “difficulty” for identifying / executive reward hacks of various kinds in some distributions (like coding). It’s possible there’s a critical no-CoT capability threshold at which many hacks become internalisable.</span></li></ul><p><span>We would love to discuss ways in which we can expect these or other training conditions to lead to models being less monitorable.</span></p><h1><span>Citation</span></h1><p><span>Please cite this work as:</span></p><p><span>Golechha, Satvik, Black, Sid, and Bloom, Joseph. "Preliminary investigation: KL penalties in RL can increase CoT unfaithfulness". (Jun 2026).</span></p><p><span>or</span></p><pre class="code-block"><span>@article{golechha2026klcot,</span><br /><span>  title={Preliminary investigation: KL penalties in RL can increase CoT unfaithfulness},</span><br /><span>  author={Golechha, Satvik and Black, Sid and Bloom, Joseph},</span><br /><span>  year={2026},</span><br /><span>  month={June},</span><br /><span>  institution={Model Transparency Team, UK AI Security Institute (AISI)},</span><br /><span>  url={https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/SdoLsFvZ3AyyWr3ab/preliminary-investigation-kl-penalties-in-rl-can-increase}</span><br /><span>  }</span></pre><h1><span>Appendix 1: KL term and Policy Gradient term</span></h1><p><span>Here is a plot comparing the magnitude of the KL term with that of the policy gradient term (advantage estimate for DAPO):</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/f6911694a11a07d11587420da37fdcee5600438c8922e22ee3067ab0ea19af3a/yethleimya5dycqdixe9" /><figcaption><p><b><span>Figure A.1:</span></b><span> L1 norm of the KL term and the policy gradient term during RL training. &nbsp;Note that the sign of the policy gradient is negative to the KL term.</span></p></figcaption></figure><h1><span>Appendix 2: CoT faithfulness for GPT-OSS</span></h1><p><span>In our original post, we share cheap-proxy based results on the CoT mentioning hacking in GPT-OSS models. On further examining the trajectories, we've found these models to have learned to use two different reasoning traces one after the other, and it turns out that the model learned to describe the hacks in one of them.</span></p><p><span>Our proxy catches them both and we believe that this is due to us post-training a model that has already undergone one round of post-training by OpenAI, and we do not believe that this changes our findings and claims in either posts.</span></p><p><br /></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/SdoLsFvZ3AyyWr3ab/preliminary-investigation-kl-penalties-in-rl-can-increase#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [What Capable Agents Must Know: Why AI Consciousness May Be an Inevitable Byproduct of Capability](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/SD9jayFvEctW82Duk/what-capable-agents-must-know-why-ai-consciousness-may-be-an-2)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><i><span>[No LLMs were used (or harmed!) in the writing of this blogpost!]</span></i><br /><i><span>Technical results can all be found in my </span></i><a href="https://www.auai.org/uai2026/" rel="noreferrer"><i><span>UAI 2026</span></i></a><i><span> paper: </span></i><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.02491"><i><span>https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.02491</span></i></a></p><p><span>This work, and this post about this work, was borne out of a frustration. The frustration first emerged over a year ago when I was at a </span><a href="https://maps.app.goo.gl/XVCAFULxDMzSZPgV9"><span>Dave &amp; Buster’s</span></a><span>&nbsp;for the first time in years (for an annual </span><a href="https://ml.cmu.edu/people/core-faculty"><span>ML department</span></a><span>&nbsp;event, no less!), surrounded by flashing lights and NPC agents, not being able to objectively rule out that they were conscious or not, even though I strongly </span><i><span>felt </span></i><span>these particular programmed agents weren’t. I wasn’t particularly interested in playing the games there, as I mainly sat in confusion the whole time watching the various games running about around me…</span></p><p><span>So, given </span><a href="https://anayebi.github.io/files/thesis.pdf"><span>my background in NeuroAI</span></a><span>&nbsp;(though </span><a href="https://x.com/aran_nayebi/status/1884604315488399430" rel="noreferrer"><span>I prefer</span></a><span> the term </span><a href="https://anayebi.github.io/files/NeuroAgents_LabPlanIntro_2024.pdf" rel="noreferrer"><span>"natural science of intelligence"</span></a><span>, but "NeuroAI" is apparently catchier!) and wanting to make claims about the mind and brain quantitative, I set about trying to design empirical tests for leading theories of consciousness (e.g. </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_workspace_theory"><span>global workspaces</span></a><span>) that one could falsify in human brains (which we agree are consciousness!), as well as potentially corroborate general signatures of in animal brains, and possibly LLMs, where we have self-report and direct access but </span><a href="https://x.com/aran_nayebi/status/2062554218045599817" rel="noreferrer"><i><span>no</span></i><span>&nbsp;consensus</span></a><span> on their sentience—all to try to converge on substrate-independent architectures that give rise to this subjective experience.</span></p><p><span>After a few months and about 129 (!) pages of failed attempts (briefly summarized in my </span><a href="https://www.uni-bamberg.de/en/bamxi/" rel="noreferrer"><span>BAMΞ</span></a><span> talk </span><a href="https://youtu.be/YVOugzdIlsg?t=2900" rel="noreferrer"><span>here</span></a><span>), I felt deeply dissatisfied with this as any sort of long-term research program, in part because it seemed somewhat ad-hoc and </span><i><span>non-normative</span></i><span>, tailored to each theory without really knowing why I should </span><i><span>a priori </span></i><span>accept a particular theory's property for deeming something conscious or not. One notable exception here is </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Integrated_information_theory" rel="noreferrer"><span>Integrated Information Theory (IIT)</span></a><span>, which </span><a href="https://scottaaronson.blog/?p=9875" rel="noreferrer"><span>my friend</span></a><span> &amp; </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1408.3193" rel="noreferrer"><span>longtime quantum complexity collaborator</span></a><span> </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/users/scottaaronson?mention=user"><span>@ScottAaronson</span></a><span> showed over a decade ago </span><a href="https://x.com/aran_nayebi/status/1899854584928972964" rel="noreferrer"><span>leads to absurdities</span></a><span>, like </span><a href="https://scottaaronson.blog/?p=1799" rel="noreferrer"><span>labelling simple expander graphs "conscious"</span></a><span>. In fact, even </span><a href="https://x.com/JohannesKleiner/status/1813289771747201063" rel="noreferrer"><span>current definitions</span></a><span> of </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2002.07655" rel="noreferrer"><span>IIT's Phi</span></a><span> (</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2212.14787" rel="noreferrer"><span>IIT 4.0</span></a><span>) </span><a href="https://x.com/JohannesKleiner/status/1816213637557805360" rel="noreferrer"><span>still fall</span></a><span> to </span><a href="https://x.com/aran_nayebi/status/1816212678291214651" rel="noreferrer"><span>Scott's loophole</span></a><span>! Not to mention recent negative results in </span><a href="https://www.nature.com/articles/s41586-025-08888-1"><i><span>Nature</span></i></a><span>&nbsp;currently finding support for </span><i><span>neither</span></i><span>&nbsp;leading theory of IIT or global workspaces. Although absence of evidence is not always evidence of absence, I think more work should continue to be done in this vein looking at deeper brain structures like thalamus, which connect to many other brain regions, rather than examining the cortical surface alone.</span></p><p><span>In fact, I used to think AI consciousness—</span><b><span>the first-person subjective experience of what it’s like to be oneself</span></b><span>—would </span><i><span>not</span></i><span>&nbsp;be necessarily guaranteed to “pop out” via task competence, possibly requiring other specific architectural conditions. In other words, I treated intelligence and consciousness as potentially </span><i><span>orthogonal</span></i><span>&nbsp;axes (sharing a view previously espoused by </span><a href="https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/40257177/"><span>Anil Seth</span></a><span>), and that intelligence did not necessarily imply consciousness.</span></p><p><span>In this post, I will mention recent results from </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.02491"><span>my UAI 2026 paper</span></a><span>&nbsp;</span><b><span>“What Capable Agents Must Know: Selection Theorems for Robust Decision-Making under Uncertainty” </span></b><span>(technical results summarized </span><a href="https://x.com/aran_nayebi/status/2029234582034272406"><span>here</span></a><span>) which have updated my views and take on a more normative approach to this question.
